The founder of Dubai, Sheik Rashid, was asked about the future of his Country, and he replied, "My grandfather rode a camel, my father rode a camel, I ride a Mercedes, my son rides a Land Rover, and my grandson is also going to ride a Land Rover. But my great-grandson is going to have to ride a camel."
"Why is that?", he was asked.
His reply was, "Hard times create strong men. Strong men create easy times. Easy times create weak men. Weak men create difficult times. Many will not understand it, but you have to raise warriors, not parasites.
Add to that the historical reality that all great empires the Persians, the Trojans, the Egyptians, the Greeks, the Romans, and in later years, the British.. all rose and perished within 240 years. 
They were not conquered by external enemies; they rotted from within. America has now passed that 240 year mark, and the rot is starting to be visible and is accelerating. We are past the Mercedes and Land Rover Years. the camels are on the horizon."
The greatest generation consisted of 18 year old kids storming the beaches at Normandy. And now, two generations later, some 18-year-old kids want to hide in safe rooms when they hear words that hurt their feelings. They also want free handouts from the government because they think they are entitled to it.
"The camels are on the horizon" for sure!

[03/05/23 11:55 PM] Dad: +GOTTA STAY BUSY AS YU AGE


When you’re young and a summer lasts forever, and the school year lasts eternity to the 10th, it’s because your perception of time is marked by new events….almost everything is new, so your brain is constantly marking events as passages of time. We cram a lot of events into short passages of time.
When we’re youth challenged, there just aren’t that many new events…it’s not new to go to work, come home, clean, go to bed, go shopping and talk to the cashier….we’ve done this thousands of times. So the event markers are spaced farther and farther apart.
So for us,, the time from Halloween to Christmas is a blink….and summer lasts only slightly longer than a sneeze. From our perspective what felt like a month turns out to be two years.
We need to mark new events…do new things. The more often we do them, the more our perception of time slows down.
iIr

Nietzsche was the one who did the job for me. At a certain moment in his life,the idea came to him of what he called 'the love of your fate.' Whatever your fate is, whatever the hell happens, you say, 'This is what I need.' It may look like a wreck, but go at it as though it were an opportunity, a challenge. If you bring love to that moment--not discouragement--you will find the strength is there. Any disaster you can survive is an improvement in your character, your stature, and your life. What a privilege! This is when the spontaneity of your own nature will have a chance to flow.

Then, when looking back at your life, you will see that the moments which seemed to be great failures followed by wreckage were the incidents that shaped the life you have now. You’ll see that this is really true. Nothing can happen to you that is not positive. Even though it looks and feels at the moment like a negative crisis, it is not. The crisis throws you back, and when you are required to exhibit strength, it comes.

[08/10/23 03:56 PM] Jordan: +Sharing a few notes/thoughts from the therapy session: 

- I will be amazed at how much energy and clarity I have on the other side of grief
- Majority of people just can’t and don’t understand how much space/energy grief takes up, and how much energy it’s taking for you to hold it in place given your overall life situation
- At an evolutionary level, your nervous system is in a deep battle with Annelies’s betrayal and abandonment
- The world is different on the other side of that
- My anxiety in Montana makes sense, (this parenthesis is my own commentary for you, I’m not angry or frustrated about this, just want to be transparent to share my feelings: hearing in the other room things you and mom say about how sensitive I am or how you never were that tired at my age is part of what makes being around people generally difficult for me right now and leads to me wanting to be alone. It makes me feel not seen or understood, and makes me want to run fast the other direction from anyone saying these things in this moment. I say I’m not angry or frustrated because of how deeply appreciate you continuing to show up for me and help me, I’m lucky you’re playing the role of my dad so well and I’m grateful. But I NEED to feel understood with little things like not wanting to go meet your friends for drinks right now, reflecting empathy and understanding of how little energy I have right now)
- Back to the notes, disassociation is a self induced semi hypnotic state, it separates part of your experience away from your awareness, you can’t be a surgeon (this isn’t related to you he doesn’t know you’re a surgeon and we weren’t talking about you) and not disassociate, high achievers are excellent at disassociation, as you’re disassociating from an experience that will cripple you if you don’t process it 
- I have not fully processed what’s happening (betrayal and abandonment)
- Next session will focus on moving forward to figure out how to process and disassociate. iI

[11/18/23 11:10 PM] Dad: +So true. It happens in many relationships where one or both partners develop such a strong emotional attachment to the other it robs you of yourself and mental health and stability. Fortunately, or unfortunately, you likely, after recovery will guard against such deep affection and dependence on another person. It may lead to a more healthy relationship with the right person or prevent you from developing a deep intimacy again. The pain from this leads to a protective survival response. You will give of yourself to another again but will never allow yourself to give up that part of your being necessary to maintain the healthy relationship with yourself. I know, I’ve experienced it and never will again. iI

[09/10/24 06:05 PM] Jordan: +Just got off the second opinion call and Dr. Li was super kind. He agrees with Dr. Chang about for sure needing the surgery and thinks I should do it in the next few months (he thought January was okay). Once it starts blocking fluid I’d get very sick, so much much better to do it before that happens. He thinks there’s some chance the tension headaches/memory gaps/brain fog is caused by the tumor even though the mri doesn’t show fluid backing up, and would push the surgery earlier if these things were getting worse. He described the risks as a bit higher than Dr chang. 10-30% chance of short term neurological issues (<3 months), 1-5% chance of long term issues, 1-2% chance of infection, <1% chance of issues with anesthesia. The neurological issues he mentioned were around balance/swallowing/limb control. He described himself as a more conservative surgeon - less likely to operate than Dr Chang which might also mean his risk assessment is a bit higher. He said Dr Chang is a very very good surgeon. He said there’s only one surgical approach to removing a tumor from the 4th ventricle, suboccipital craniotomy, so he agreed with Dr Chang there as well. He said I’d be able to shower/use the restroom by myself but would need someone to cook/drive/lift things for me for a few weeks. I forgot to ask about stairs.  He thinks there’s some low chance it is an ependymoma and if it wasn’t fully removed I might need radiation (but unlikely)iI

[04/04/25 02:46 PM] Dad: +}So, we turn to Global X S&P 500 Covered Call ETF (XYLD), which yields more than ten timesthe dividend yield. XYLD owns the same stocks as SPY, but it also writes covered calls on the index itself.

XYLD currently has written calls on SPY that expire on April 17. When that happens, the fund will write new calls for May—and more income.

The income from these constantly expiring calls is the key to XYLD’s sky-high “synthetic yield.” The fund collects premiums from option buyers immediately after it writes these calls, generating steady income for shareholders.

We can think of this as “renting” out positions to generate extra cash. XYLD owns the underlying shares behind the S&P 500. Each month it rents out its collection of stocks and collects the option premiums. Rinse and repeat.I THINK THIS IS A BUY UP TO 41.50. Watch it today as it may go down a bit more.iI

[08/29/25 04:04 PM] Dad: +When a narcissistic person is blocked from manipulating and their attempts are resisted, the expected reaction typically involves anger, defensiveness, and attempts to escalate their behavior to regain control.
Common Responses
	•	Rage and Retaliation: Narcissists often experience narcissistic injury and may react with intense anger or even try to punish, embarrass, or get revenge on the person resisting them.
	•	Denial and Deflection: They frequently deny any wrongdoing, reverse blame, justify their actions, and refuse to accept responsibility. Tactics may include gaslighting and invalidation.
	•	Victimization: Some narcissists portray themselves as victims and may engage in smear campaigns to gain sympathy from others and maintain their self-image.
	•	Manipulation Escalation: When manipulation fails, the narcissist may escalate with more dramatic or desperate tactics, including emotional outbursts, attempts to bait responses, or trying to provoke emotional engagement.
	•	Passive Aggression: Withdrawal, silent treatment, or other forms of passive aggression are also common as they seek to punish or destabilize the person who resists.
Impact and How to Respond
	•	The narcissist’s main goal is to regain control, so their actions are meant to provoke emotional reactions, confusion, and self-doubt in the person resisting them.
	•	Staying calm, emotionally detached, and refusing to engage with manipulation can help protect mental well-being and prevent escalation.
	•	Consistently enforced boundaries are key, as narcissists frequently test limits and will intensify efforts if boundaries are inconsistent.
Overall, resisting narcissistic manipulation typically triggers aggressive, disruptive, or manipulative behaviors as an attempt to restore control or punish the targetiI

[10/27/25 09:30 PM] Dad: +How to stay fit as you get older


I’m 88. I’m a Marine Corps veteran which got me a good start. But you don’t really need that now
	1	Drink water. Plain old cold tap water is fine. Let your body tell you how much.
	2	So, listen to your body. It’ll tell you when to eat, to poop, to anything. If your body’s against it, don’t do it.
	3	Walk, never run, at least 3 times a week for one hour. Don’t push your body. Just go out to a park and walk.
	4	Don’t suffer fools. Period. Just don’t put up with stupidity or ignorance in your life. They’ll try to change you. Don’t let them.
	5	Don’t worry about money! Please.
	6	Have a good time, tell some jokes, listen to others. Laugh, a lot.

[11/02/25 02:09 AM] Jordan: +Hey Clark! As long as you trust the person you are seeing, I think that’s the most important. My thoughts:

CTI ablations are very successful for typical flutter - almost 95% without recurrence. PVI ablations for afib are known to have much lower success rates - 70-80%. Not only that, but afib is common after pvi ablation which is why all the afib studies include a blanking period for 90 days after the procedure (where they don’t “count” recurrent afib as a failed PVI ablation if it happens within 90 days of the ablation because it’s “expected” due to injuring the posterior wall of the left atrium during the ablation itself.) It would suck to develop symptomatic afib within 90 days of the PVI ablation, when you don’t have a clear indication for the procedure.

As long as you trust who you are seeing I say go for it. But if you just have flutter - just getting a CTI ablation might be a reasonable option. And if in 10 years you get afib, you can get the PVI then. PVIs are pretty easy overall. 

Lastly, this is for TYPICAL flutter. Atypical flutter is a different story, and if you actually have afib that is also a different story. 

Sorry to hear you are dealing with this - hope you are doing well otherwise. 
McHaleiI

[12/05/25 10:28 PM] Dad: +nhave worked for billionaires, centimillionaires and still represent mainly the affluent. What I see with extremely wealthy people that have created or grown a business,senior executives or entrepreneurs, rather than inherited wealth is the time commitment to effectively run any company of any magnitude and stay current on how your funds are being invested have very little time available outside of business commitments. The business and financial part of your life is an enormously time consuming task. The paradox is that with the increased commitment to your business, the decreased time available for your personal life. The expression of stopping to smell the roses, is applicable.
Think of it, you can purchase anything you ever dreamed of, but have little time to enjoy it. There are always exceptions, but that has been my observation. Most wealthy people who started their own businesses or executives who worked their way to the “C” suite, senior executives, are the first to work, the last to leave and turn off the lights and coffee machine before they leave.
The pay is great, but do not forget to smell the roses!
iIj

[02/20/26 02:17 PM] Dad: I think the window is more like 3-6 weeks.  At 3 weeks the natural diminution of tissue quality after injury is maximized and between 3-6 weeks is the sweet spot.  Some surgeons would define 2 weeks as the ideal time.  Early fibrosis starts occurring at 2 weeks so thus the 3 week time frame.  not sure what AI says but that is my opinion after 45 yrs of treating injuries.
[02/20/26 02:21 PM] Dad: Yes, I think the sooner the better with the MRI so you know what yu are dealing with.  I think i my experience taking into account the nature of the injury, 2 weeks would be ideal contingent on the details of the injury itself, ie clean detachment without significant injury to adjacent structures, but I would say 4 weeks would be the next ideal time frame if additional tissue damage is obvious on MRI

[03/04/26 12:09 AM] Dad: +Problem:

With so few opportunities to pull permits it has become a challenge to maintain the stoke for boating.
Even when trips become available during peak season, the river corridors are so overrun with people combined with the push for first come camps its hard to say it feels like a wilderness experience.

Solution:
1) Boat in the shoulder or off season. It takes more gear and planning but that it why I back pack.

2) Find new rivers w/o permits. These will include extended travel times and complex logistics, small airplanes, and hefty expenses but they are out there. Find someone that has experience with breakdown frames hint, I know a guy. Limiting dry boxes giant heavy items will also help.

3) trade river time for a new activity that gives you the same since of adventure. For me, its flying. Getting the skills to do fly in camping trips is a commitment but well worth the investment. Most people gravitate toward the backcountry flying in places like idaho but there are many less committing options out there as well. Example: San Juan islands. Multiple airports have campgrounds with camping under the wing.

If you have kids between 8-18, check out EAA.org and look for their Young Eagle's program. It a fway to expose kids to general aviation. I have done a few of these and its fun for all involved. The other option is find a flight school and ask for a discovery flight. 

Keep doing the snow dances and lets hope for a miracle March.iI

I get 31.54 per share which if option expires reduces my cost to 275-31.54 or 243.00 and I keep the shares.
